ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e210	No one has asked for the floor.
ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e224	Let me start this account by quoting one of my predecessors, Per Berg Lund. When he spoke to the Stortinge during the March 1931 budgetary procedure, he said the following: ‘I think it may be useful to recall, however, that since the government set up its draft budget in November, the economic and financial situation in this country is undoubtedly weaker, thereby making the chances and prospects for the future darker.’ [[...]] [[...]] When we wanted to welcome a new decade a few months ago, we were completely unaware of what is now defining most of everything we are doing, and the decisions we are making – not least the revised budget that we are presenting today. It is exactly two months since we introduced the strictest and most incisive measures we have had in peacetime in Norway — two months. Today we know that we may have to live with infection in society for two years. Even with the experience we've been doing over the past few weeks, it's hard to see exactly how it's going to develop, and what consequences it's going to have. The Korona crisis is a global pandemic. It affects life and health and has a major impact on the economy and jobs. The uncertainty is the most defining for the time we live in right now. For many, it's just the uncertainty that's the heaviest thing to bear. It's in the nature of man to look for the answers we don't have. In the situation we're in, it's time to answer how long we have to live like this, answer what works, what challenges we've dealt with well – and what we could do better. At the same time, uncertainty will become an even heavier should and the consequences of the crisis more serious for every day that goes, until the world again is as we know it. Time is both our best friend and our worst enemy. Is there an answer to the story? In peacetime, we have to go all the way back to the 1930s to find an economic shock near what we're experiencing now. The most important lesson from this is that economic policy is an important and powerful instrument that can help people and businesses in crisis. In recent times, the backlash in 1987 has made the deepest mark. The decline led to high unemployment and the banking crisis at the beginning of the 1990s. Then, among other things, we learned: the importance of solid banks, an experience that made us better equipped to face the storm when the financial crisis hit in 2008. The financial crisis, originating in the United States and global financial markets, also created high unemployment and recession in large parts of the Western world. And throughout the decade we just left behind, the aftereffects were like a heavy blanket over economic development, especially in Europe. From the financial crisis, we learned that it is effective to use economic policy quickly and vigorously, and in times like now, we see the value of the international work that jumped out of the financial crisis, in the supervision and regulation of financial markets. The 2014–2015 oil crisis also taught us something, including: the importance of targeted and temporary actions. At the time, the measures contributed to the activity and to stopping what could have become a negative spiral. How deep this crisis becomes, we don't know. We estimate that the value creation in mainland Norway is decreasing by 4 pst. This year, but the case may also be deeper. In many industries, the activity has stopped in whole or in part. One out of seven persons in the labour force is on leave or unemployed, either in whole or in part. Families and communities are hit. This crisis is different from the previous, and it is both steeper and deeper. For every crisis we've been through, we've developed new tools for our toolbox. So some answers are to be found in history, but at the same time it clearly shows that each crisis has its own peculiarity. That's why it doesn't just keep building on the experience and tools we already have. We have to think for ourselves, we have to think for ourselves, and we have to think for ourselves. We need to develop new tools, and the tools we have need to adapt the image we see in front of us, not behind us. The strict control measures were necessary to control the infection, but they gave immediate and drastic results in the economy. We had a bad time. For the first four weeks, we got new crisis response every week. Let me take this opportunity to thank those who made it possible, and there are many, among them the Stortinget, the social partners, business organisations, the ministries and the directorates. The size ensured rapid handling of cases, but has also contributed to new proposals and strengthened government action. In these weeks we have really known the value of trust – and the power of cooperation: that what seems almost impossible becomes possible when we move in the same direction. It will even be possible from a home office in the living room. The very first, acute phase was largely concerned with creating safety for workers and businesses: Permitting legislation was amended in such a way that a greater proportion of the pay obligation is assumed by the State and the prospective benefits are improved in terms of security of income. Tax receipts were deferred — and taxes were reduced — in order to improve the liquidity of enterprises both in the mainland and on the pedestal over a demanding period. A State loan guarantee scheme for small and medium-sized enterprises was established. At record speed, a broad and comprehensive compensation scheme for firms in difficulty has been put in place. We have also implemented targeted measures for, inter alia: growth companies, train companies, aviation, culture, volunteering, sport, brewery, process industry, students and apprentices. Today, in the revised national budget, we are presenting new measures – measures that contribute to activity across the country, and security and support to those who need it: Strong municipalities and county municipalities are essential for good schools, welfare services and activity throughout the country. The outbreak of the virus has also affected them financially. Income has decreased, and expenditure has increased, especially for infectious diseases and health care. We have already adopted various measures to compensate for this, and today we are proposing even more. Lower expected price and salary growth will also strengthen the finances of municipalities. Overall, this means that the municipal sector is compensated for the effects of the virus outbreak, as the picture now looks – but the picture may change, and there may be a need to do more. The government will set up a working group with the KS, and the group will monitor the impact of the coronary crisis on the municipal sector. We're proposing a 500-million package of skills. kr to higher education and vocational training places. This is an important investment in our youth and future competent labour force. The initiative is the first part of the ‘Education Commitment 2020’ competence package, which will be reinforced by several actions in a few weeks’ time. In the last few months, many people have learned what opportunities it gives the school with digital tools. We want to make it possible for digital education to have a central place in school in the future, and therefore propose a further 140 million. kr for further bet on this. We're proposing 400 million. to a separate package of measures for vulnerable children and young people, for closer follow-up of students who have lost valuable education and training, and for increased commitment to health care and activities for those with high needs. The society that abruptly shuts down must be ready to support children and young people when it is gradually reopened. We're proposing to give health companies an increased room for action of 6 billion. kr, such that among other things: doctors and nurses can continue the important work they do. We also want to raise the frame to Nav by 700 million. kr, such that among other things: those who are now on leave and unemployed receive the income insurance they need. To compensate for the loss of revenue in public transport and Avinor, we propose 1.5 billion. DKK to counties and up to SEK 4,3 billion. kr to Avinor. We follow up the Storting's request decision on a proposal of 920 million. kr for media compensation, volunteering, non-profit institutions and businesses, inter alia. culture and museum operations. We follow up the Storting decision on a temporary adjustment of the housing aid scheme. The change will result in some 13 000 new households receiving housing support and more for those already in the scheme. We also follow up on the decision on the reduction of the employer’s contribution for May and June. The measure is equal to 8 billion. kr. And we're proposing $300 million. kr in increased capital to Nysnø. This will give the company greater opportunity to invest in companies with promising climate technologies. As previously notified, we are also presenting a separate notification of measures that will improve the liquidity of the oil industry by up to 100 billion. kr, to mitigate the fall in activity which may result from a low oil price. Combined with tax deferrals and falling income from the base, all these measures have reversed the government finances of the year. In the fall we budgeted with a surplus of 255 billion. kr, including oil income and fund income. Now we can get a deficit of 124 billion. kr. At the same time, it is important to remember that the option of using powerful economic instruments is now likely to have led to even more bankruptcy and even higher unemployment. It would have been an even greater price to pay for us as a society. But also in times of crisis, we have a responsibility to look after money usage. In the future, new economic measures cannot only come to the top of existing ones. Some measures also need to be stepped down and phased out, as it may take a long time for economic activity to recover properly. We put extra aside in good times. That's why we can also use more when we're in a serious crisis. This is entirely in line with the intention of the rule of action. But the rule of action also says that when the economy goes back to normal, money use needs to be reduced. Oil revenues shall benefit several generations. Norwegian government finances are not a Sarepta pot. We should have had more time to figure out the expensive and comprehensive arrangements we have put in place over the past few weeks. We didn't have that time, because no one was prepared for how great the economic consequences would be – not us, not business. A company does not normally have large amounts of savings in the event of a pandemic. An enterprise invests money in equipment, goods and growth – growth that creates jobs. Therefore, enterprises cannot survive for months without income, perhaps not even weeks. Norwegian business is diverse, and each enterprise is unique, whether it is: farming of fish in the north supply or travel establishments in the west; processing industry in the south forestry in the country of origin; or a hairdresser or a builder in Oslo. It is not possible to put in place measures that are equally effective for all. So there will be someone who gets less support than they ideally should, and someone who gets more. The aim of the arrangements we have put in place is to ensure that as many healthy and viable businesses as possible get through the crisis well. When I compare different crises, I try to see the big picture. But in our policies, the way we deal with the crisis, we need to look at how the crisis affects individuals and individual industries. The reality is that it is the choices of the individual — on its own behalf, or on behalf of the enterprise — that determine the future course of development. People have spent less money now, and many save more. The consumption will probably pick up as shops and restaurants open again. But how much they want to spend depends on how they themselves assess the future of their own economy, how strongly the uncertainty feels. The same applies to the enterprises. With all the unforeseeableness that characterizes the world now, there is reason to believe that many will postpone investment. Over time, the economy will change, but the transition phase is demanding, and it becomes more demanding over time. The depth and duration of the decline will depend to a large extent on how long we need to maintain infection prevention measures — not only in Norway but also internationally. It is positive for the Norwegian economy and workplaces that many infectious diseases measures are now softening. But we have to expect that the activity can be lower than normal over a long period of time. We can't avoid that. We are not just dependent on the situation in Norway. What's happening outside our borders is striking our economy to a great extent. Our trading partners will together have a decrease in the value creation of 6 pst. This year, according to the IMF. Then there is little drag help to get for our big and important export industries. And as long as the oil price seems to be low, it will weigh on oil investment and thus the activity of the supplier industries, industries which employ thousands of people along the coast, and which are on a competence that is – and will be – important to Norway. It doesn't help much to shut down the virus here at home if it keeps spreading beyond our limits. We can't or will not live with closed borders forever. And even though we can soften our own infectious disease prevention measures, the way out of the crisis will be marked by the fact that large parts of our business are part of international value chains. We're fighting an enemy that doesn't know national boundaries. We must fight that fight together. We are a small country that has built value creation and welfare on trade in a densely interwoven world. We have discovered, extracted and sold oil in a world market that has been increasingly organized for trade across nations and regions. We have participated in an adventurous development that has given better access to goods, hands, knowledge and competences. Never before have these possibilities been so great, for so many. But already before this crisis we saw trends in trade barriers, trade wars and ‘first ourselves’. Yes, the crisis has revealed vulnerabilities by being dependent on well-functioning world trade. We have to deal with the vulnerabilities, and they have to be handled. Nevertheless, this is not the time to risk a reversal of cooperation that has taken decades to build up. We need an international effort, both to deal with the pandemic and to limit the economic impact, and to prevent new pandemics. It is particularly important that the international community does not forget the most vulnerable. Low income countries are hit hard. For this reason, the government proposes that Norway, among other things, should: contribute to disaster funds and loan schemes for low-income countries, under the auspices of the World Bank and the IMF. This is in addition to contributions to the UN Corona Fund and to the development of vaccines. History has taught us how powerful the dynamics of economic trade are. There is a strong link between policies that promote economic growth and trade and policies that aim at peace, stability and well-being. Countries that trade with each other are less wary. When economic ties are tight, countries have a lot to lose on getting into conflict. In the mid-war period, the market fell as a result of protectionist measures. This contributed to a deep economic downturn. The aim of free trade is access to goods and services: imports so that we can buy technology developed and produced on other continents, while people in other parts of the world get the joy of Norwegian fish for dinner. In order to import, we have to export. We've become a richer country because we could export fish, timber, shipping services, oil and gas. Today we export 13 billion seafood meals a year, to a value of about 110 billion. kr. Fish are the basis of some of our most important district jobs. At the same time, last year we exported oil and gas for a total of 450 billion. kr. But free trade is not only important for the jobs on the base or in the coastal districts; free trade is important for jobs throughout our country. We rely on access to raw materials, on technology, on labour, and we rely on competition, to continue to improve so that we can meet tomorrow's market. Now, if globalisation were to reverse, it would be a setback to the world order that has been shown throughout the post-war period to contribute to the transformation and welfare of the world, which has lifted millions of people out of poverty. Our own history, our economy and our social structure oblige Norway to take responsibility for contributing to cooperation, trade and a regular world order. We owe that to our fellow citizens and our descendants. Minister of Finance Lund continues his statement in 1931 as follows: «Even if you never eat that much, it has been difficult to really discover the points of light that should herald a improvement – but I think that the prospects for the very near future here in the country are undoubtedly much darker. We must also be aware that in the current future it will result in a deterioration in the state's revenue, which will lead to a deficit in the budget, and we will also be aware that the consequences of such a situation will cast its shadows far into the future. [[...]] [[...]] Just as the power of a crisis is just as raw and brutal as it hits the individual, the way out of the crisis will be just as powerful as the sum of our ambitions – almost 5.5 million people with individual goals, will and action. Together, we will take Norway through this crisis and into what will become our new everyday life. The economic measures taken so far are largely adapted to a rather short-term crisis. They are targeted and will be temporary. Although we have been a little over the last few weeks, there are still some forms of activity that we do not want, for example. that very many people gather in a limited area. The measures to limit activity must lead to what economic measures we are taking in the future and when they are implemented. Otherwise, financial measures may counteract the effectiveness of the disease protection measures. We can't press both the brake and the gas at the same time. We probably have to live for a while. So in months to come, we have to get the wheels back on track, while at the same time we have control of the infection. We need to phase out some of the financial measures we have put in place and find solutions that contribute to jobs and activity. We have to avoid having unemployment snapped. Dropping out of work can have consequences for the rest of your life. And for every person who falls outside, we, as a society, also lose valuable skills and labour. Everyone has something to contribute. Our contribution must be to facilitate that companies have strong incentives to hire, secure bets and space to grow. In a few weeks the government will come back to Stortinget with a proposition about the way out of the crisis. It needs to be about new measures, which are being put in place at the right time, are on the right scale, act quickly and support in the context of green transition and growth. We also need to be able to adapt the plans continuously; the terrain can change quickly. Then the map must also be adjusted. We have experienced that in the last few months, and that uncertainty is still present. As part of this work, the government has extended its mandate to the expert group on the socio-economic impact of disease prevention measures. The group shall include, inter alia: consider how financial measures should be organised in order to contribute to a good transition out of the crisis. The success of stepping down the crisis response and facilitating growth and employment is crucial to ensuring sustainable welfare systems in the coming years. Expenditure in general government was already at a high level before this crisis. We must avoid them becoming permanently higher when the crisis is over, avoiding State capital replacing private capital. People should return to their jobs in private business, not be employed in the public sector. In any case, we will have to deal with the aftermath of the crisis for a long time in the future. It will make the challenges we had before the virus outbreak, even bigger when this is over. Therefore, we need to find ways to get through this crisis without losing sight of the long-term perspectives. We've known for a long time that we can't borrow as much of the revenue from the oil business in the future. The room for increasing the use of oil money will be smaller than before. Fortunately, oil wealth is not our greatest asset. It's the people who live here, the labour force. Knowledge and competence are the most valuable things we can invest in. The need for more knowledge, increased competence, isn't about everyone else, it's about us all. We have to spend time in the future in a good way. We know that the future will require more and new skills for jobseekers, employees and businesses. That is why we want to invest more in education and skills. We know that the future requires green conversion and cut emissions. Therefore, we will invest in new technologies that can contribute to this. Almost every day we hear about occupations that disappear, that we have a working life changing, and that automation comes with stormscrat. At the same time, technological development is only one of the factors leading to major changes in Norwegian working life. All major trends – demographic trends, increasing pension expenditure, globalisation, climate change and increased competition, together with the pandemic – tell us one thing: working life is changing. We need to have good, and not least correct, skills in the future. Let me go back to the 1930s. In 1931 Thomas Edison must have told his good friend Henry Ford: I would have put my money in solar energy. For an energy source! I hope we don't have to wait until it's empty for oil and coal until we get there. – The global community is facing a huge challenge in reducing greenhouse gas emissions. The government has high ambitions in climate policy. We have taken important action. It takes something to transform society in a green direction, it costs a lot to prevent climate risk – but investment is worth it, and with innovation and efficient use of resources, we can achieve economic growth that is both high and green. We are well-informed with a plan for Norway's way out of the crisis. At the same time, we have to raise our eyes even further. We need good analysis of how the Norwegian and international economies are affected by the crisis. That is why we have today set up a selection of competent people with different backgrounds, practical and academic, and from different parts of the country. Together, they will provide us with their best description of Norway towards 2025, analyses and important input into further efforts to facilitate tomorrow’s business and green transition, in the light of the consequences of the pandemic, both in Norway and internationally. Fortunately, we have had the outbreak under control, but with regard to the economy, the greatest trials can still lie ahead of us. It's going to be demanding, for us as a society, for individuals and for fellow humans. In our lifetime, the Norwegian economy has largely sailed in downwind. Now the wind's turned. We've got an impact page. When the whole world community has to go through a deep recession, there's little drag help to get out. Much stands and therefore falls upon ourselves this time. Fortunately, we may have the world's best starting point for success. We still have solid government finances, a highly educated population, a resilient business, good welfare arrangements and high trust in each other. How well we succeed, however, depends on how good we are to choose the right actions, and whether we are able to stand in the transition. On the way to the new normal, some businesses will be lost. Others may want to be kept alive artificially for a while, with the help of the state. Someone will survive because they get the help they need. And new businesses and industries will grow, including: those who dare to seize the opportunities and fill the space that is now emerging – new markets. Private business needs to change and growth needs to come, such quantities of small industrial enterprises emerging during the crisis years of the 1930s and growing viable. It may not have been so obvious just when it happened, but it became very clear after how new ideas and new working methods helped to lift the country out of the economic mess. There is a change that provides the basis for new progress. If we manage to bring along the good that is emerging in this crisis, we can get strengthened out of this: a significant step closer to a more diverse, competitive, digitised and greener society that creates more, and that includes more.
ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e426	The President will now, according to Storting’s Rules of Procedure, § 45, open for a short comment round, limited to one post of up to 5 minutes from each party group and final statements from the Council of State.
ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e431	First, I would like to thank the Minister of Finance for the work over two very demanding months for our country. Nothing is more important than the fact that workers, families and businesses get the most through this crisis. There is no economic policy that can prevent coronary eruptions. What economic policy can do is dampen the setback by keeping the economy moving and taking care of people who need it. If people's lives go down and no one is there – no safety net, no welfare scheme, no income insurance – then the crisis will be longer both for them personally and for us as a society. For this reason, the Labour Party has been particularly concerned about securing social justice and jobs in the negotiations we have had on crisis packages in the past. There is also some reason why we were a little surprised when the Minister of Labour and Social Affairs said today that ‘being able to get a lot of people back to work now has little to do with it’. This sounds quite passive. Our job as elected people now is to get the economy back on track and get people back to work. The Labour Party is concerned that this crisis should intensify some dangerous developments in our society that we had seen in the years before: increasing economic differences between the poor and the rich, and that many had no job to go to. For this reason, we have sidelined party policy and have had wide-ranging negotiations on the crisis response. On the road, the Labour Party will cooperate across the political parties when it is in the best interests of the country. However, party-political differences are no longer present. The budget has now been submitted for revision, late last year, after seven years of under-financing of our hospitals, up to the strictest municipal settlement in fifteen years. The municipalities are an important part of the security network of people, and KS has estimated an economic need as a result of the corona pandemic that equals up to 20 billion. kr. This year, we need to do what we can to protect our elderly and health professionals who are to provide good care and care, and now that society is to be gradually reopened, this is particularly important because of the risk of infection. The people in the front line – the laymen, the nurses, the health workers – have received a lot of applause for the job they have done, but now they also have to get the jobs and basic staff they do. Now that the national budget is revised, action can be taken. Several hundred thousand are out of work or out of work, and in the future, work becomes the number one job. Then it is clear that the companies need help to survive. At the moment, there is a special trade in goods and services that have been delayed, but we also know that the oil, gas and supply industries are in a very stressful situation. The Labour Party looks forward to negotiating solutions for the industry, focusing on securing jobs in Norway, securing revenue from the state and making necessary climate-friendly investments. In addition, the supplier industry must be assured that there will be significant State-initiated investment in, among others. electrification, hydrogen, sea wind and CO 2 capture and storage. There's nothing that can happen in a few years, it has to happen now. Workers need to know that contracts are coming, and companies need to know that the strongest of Norwegian traditions, where trade unions, industry and authorities work together, will be preserved in the future. Regardless of the industry or the company, it also has something to say about what kind of jobs people go back to. Is it fixed, whole, or is it temporary lease-in contracts? Are they going back to a work life with a high degree of organisation or to workplaces where the trade unions are weak or absent? For the Workers' Party, it is quite clear that we are forced to create a fairer Norway, where more people get the opportunity to work, where firm, whole settings are norms, where trade unions are strong, and where no one stands alone. That's what we're going to work for -- that's what we're going to do. It's a time for everything. There will be a time for evaluation. That time is not now. But it is now time to have a direct and honest debate on the economic priorities we should do, and the revision of the national budget is the natural place to take that discussion. The Labour Party would like to welcome you. We look forward to constructive debates, where we will provide solutions for more social justice and job creation for the future.

The measures taken in Norway, combined with a significant fall in the international economy, together with a sharp fall in oil prices, have hit us hard. This has a major impact on the Norwegian economy and affects us all. On 12 March the Government launched the most intrusive measures in Norway in peacetime. The measures were taken to reduce the spread of the corona virus and to allow time to build up sufficient capacity in the healthcare system. Hospitals and community health services have been the spearhead in the fight against the virus. In the last few weeks, the Norwegian health system has proven itself from its very best – and it costs. That is why I am pleased that the Norwegian health care system is compensated for by 6 billion dollars. kr. It is also good that our health services, who have worked day and night for us to be safe, are compensated. I am also pleased that the government prioritises vulnerable children, who need extra follow-up. 400 million. Dr, to improve health care for our children and young people during the summer, and activities at school and during the summer holidays, will be important for our most vulnerable children. Nav is compensated for by 700 million. kr. Nav's efforts have been crucial to ensuring the income of workers in a troubled and uncertain situation. It also makes us maintain some of the purchasing power of our economy, but most importantly, it maintains the security of the individual and their family. Everyone affected by the crisis lives in a municipality — but everyone who takes the measures also lives in a municipality. Now the municipalities have been compensated, and are to be compensated, together with up to 11 billion. kr, if we add up what's happened, and what's being warned. Although estimates are uncertain, it is important to do so now. The value creation in the Norwegian economy has fallen sharply. Large parts of our business are very hard hit. Many people know the uncertainty as to whether the job or life's work is still there when the crisis is over. The way back is going to be long, but the sum of the measures we have taken, and what is coming today, is a step in the right direction. Postponements and cuts in taxes have been both important and correct. It has provided liquidity and predictability to businesses. It is necessary for the individual to also know that he or she has a job to go back to. It has been necessary for them to know that they can get over the crack and help rebuild the country again when this is over. During the last few weeks, Norwegian policies and colleagues here at Stortinget have shown their best interest. We have often agreed on important steps to ensure the safety of individuals and the liquidity of Norwegian businesses. This effort will continue when we start work on raising the Norwegian economy. Many of the measures that have been taken and will be taken cost and cost. But if we hadn't done these measures, the fall could have been deeper. The availability could be greater, the bankruptcy more and the way back further. We who sit here have the following problems: we have to keep the infection down, get people to work and take back their everyday lives.

I have not yet seen the Norwegian Minister for Foreign Affairs comment on this strong Hungarian reaction, but I am open that I may have overlooked something. ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e1232	The issue we are now dealing with is a proposal for a representative on the labelling of goods coming from areas occupied by Israel. The debate on how to deal with Israeli-produced goods, and in particular goods from occupied areas, is not new, and the opinions have been partly strong. It is quick to end up in old, rehearsed tracks when a matter like this is being discussed. I hope we avoid it in the hall here today. This is not a debate for or against the boycott of Israel; it is a debate on whether consumers should be allowed to make informed choices, not only about health, the economy, the environment and social conditions, but also about ethical considerations and international legal obligations. What is new with this proposal, compared with proposals we have discussed before, is that it is based on a judgment of the EU Court of Justice of 2019. The judgment interprets the food information system on the compulsory labelling of foodstuffs and states that products from a territory occupied by Israel must be labelled with both territorial origin and that the product originates from an Israeli settlement. The decision of the Court of Justice of the European Union is in line with the EU Commission’s 2015 interpretative opinion relating to existing legislation on the labelling of origin and the practice of the legislation on goods from Israeli settlements in areas outside the 1967 borders. The Committee is aware that the case-law of the Court of Justice of the European Union is not formally binding on Norway, but the Minister of Foreign Affairs writes to the Committee in a letter dated 17 February 2019 that such practices are nevertheless given great importance. In its comments on the matter, the Committee unanimously referred to the Foreign Minister’s opinion: ‘The purpose-based interpretation of the EEA Agreement, as well as the obligation of loyalty, normally requires uniform interpretation and application of the rules.’ This is a matter that has been under discussion in Stortinget for a long time, and as the case-rapporteur, I think it is appropriate to point out that it took a very long time for us to get the government's assessment of this proposal. It could have been understandable that the government took the time to make a proper assessment of the relevance of this judgment to Norwegian law, so that the Stortinget could adopt this proposal with the support of the government parties. Frankly, I thought that was the cause of the long treatment period. Instead, after several months, we received a page and a half long letter informing us that the question is available for government treatment. EFTA has a free trade agreement with Israel since 1992 and it follows from Article 32 of the Agreement that it applies to the territories of the Contracting Parties. With regard to what is considered the Israeli territory, it is based on the internationally recognised borders of Israel. That means the territory under Israeli control before 4 June 1967. Thus, the labelling of goods from the occupied territory does not imply any kind of boycott or breach of the 1992 free trade agreement. It is quite surprising that the government parties choose to use the term ‘exported from Israel originating from the West Bank’ without at all mentioning that the West Bank is not part of Israel but Palestinian territories occupied by Israel in violation of international law and a number of UN Security Council resolutions. That approach is particularly strange when we read the Granavolden platform and the government's expressed ambition of a balanced approach to the Middle East conflict. In the opinion of the Labour Party, if Norway is left out with the rest of Europe in terms of the labelling of goods from occupied areas, it would appear very unbalanced. In 2014, Minister of Foreign Affairs Børge Brende was interviewed by the NRK Dagsrevyen on a labelling scheme for goods from areas occupied by Israel. He said: ‘A few weeks ago, I took the initiative of an investigation in the Ministry of Foreign Affairs to look at a possible introduction. It is complex, but at the same time important to send signals that we do not accept the occupation on the West Bank. And I also have to add that the free trade agreement that was mentioned does not apply to occupied areas." He further stated: ‘I can promise that this investigation will not take eight years.’ Now it's been six years. It's not eight, but six years is also long. The European Court of Justice has clarified its rules, and the case is over-crowded. I'll bring this up with the minority proposal.

I can say that, which also believes that Norway should not be a member of the EU, but right here the EU actually shows its way. I record that the reservations made in the comments of the government parties are essentially about a little formal light, that Norway is not obliged to comply with this decision of the Court of Justice of the European Union, and to a small extent emphasises the political conditions that are actually the reason for this. In fact, the situation in the Middle East has escalated to the worse. Prime Minister Netanyahu now calls for annexation of the West Bank. So it's an enhancement of an occupation situation. This is supported by the United States. The fact that, with the support of the United States, Israel does not respect international law is quite legitimate for the world community to protest and also impose smart sanctions against, in the same way that world society, the EU and Norway participated in imposing smart sanctions against Russia after the annexation of Crimea. It is therefore quite legitimate, and I support the idea of imposing smart sanctions on a country that is fundamentally violating international law. It is a mantra that international law is Norway's most important defence. To strengthen and safeguard Norway’s sovereignty and security, agreements between countries must be respected. Those who then look through the Israeli violation of international law here not only weaken the cause of the Palestinians, but also weaken Norway's cause, because international law and strengthening it are vitally important in a large and broad context.

ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e1363	Let me first thank the case rapporteur, Marianne Marthinsen, for her work on the suspension and the Minister for Foreign Affairs of the Department's written briefing on matters relating to the SV's draft representative. Under changing governments, Norway has never found it appropriate to address such changes but, on the contrary, have a balanced approach to the Middle East conflict. After the Hamas militia took power in the Gaza Strip in 2005, the Palestinian territories were, in effect, divided both politically and geographically. Palestinian self-government agencies have run the West Bank, while Hamas has run the Gaza Strip, and Hamas will neither be disarmed nor have peace with Israel. The Christian People's Party's view of the Middle East conflict is known, and it has been followed up by us further both in government position and in opposition. We want to promote fair peace and reconciliation, and have for decades supported the work of a peace agreement, as Israel succeeded in relation to two of its Arab neighbours, Egypt and Jordan. Unilateral action does not resolve the conflict; a peace agreement is needed. It requires painful compromises. President Clinton arranged for it all around the turn of the century, and others have tried later. A solution requires good and strong leadership on both sides, with authorities capable of making commitments that are followed up and respected. It is this that enables all, both Israelis and Palestinians, Jews as well as Christians and Muslims to be guaranteed respect for their rights, and that their children can live in peace and cooperation. That goal must not be lost in a short-term game of attention, political power, rivalry and the intransigence of extremists.
ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e1388	I think the speeches so far in the debate well illustrate the complexity of this matter. For several years and under changing governments, the Norwegian authorities have taken a balanced view of the Israeli-Palestinian conflict. Our basic positions are based on a broad, cross-political consensus. The main lines of the Norwegian engagement policy are determined, including under this government. It is only a negotiated two-state solution that can create lasting peace between the parties. The government considers the Israeli settlements on the occupied territory to be international law-enforcement, and we express that at regular intervals. They constitute an obstacle to a negotiated two-state solution. The Norwegian position is enshrined in resolutions of the UN Security Council and in the judgment of the International Court of Justice in The Hague. The UN Security Council has asked the UN Member States to refrain from providing assistance to Israel in relation to the Israeli settlements. The International Court of Justice in The Hague has held that third countries have an obligation not to support their settlements. For Norway, this implies an obligation not to apply support measures and to counteract activities that support illegal settlements. This commitment has been followed up by the Norwegian side in several ways, including: by delimiting the geographical scope of the EFTA Free Trade Agreement with Israel, which has been the subject of several. At the same time, neither these governments nor previous governments have found it appropriate to introduce mandatory consumer-oriented labelling of goods from the Israeli settlements. As the rapporteurs point out, such a label scheme has been the subject of debate for several years. Proposals have been put down in the Stortinget for several rounds in the last time – in December 2017 and by June 2019 at the latest. It is an expression of the complexity of the matter. Norway has close, long-term and friendly relations with both Israelis and Palestinians. The parties see us as impartial and consistent. We have high confidence from key actors in the conflict. The fact that Norway is considered to be a predictable partner has been essential in order to contribute to the overall work of a peace agreement and a two-state solution. A broad network of contacts and close cooperation with the parties has supported our long-standing leadership of the donor countries group for Palestine, AHLC. Through the donor country group, we work to strengthen the institutional and economic basis of the two-state solution. It is the only body where the parties, together with donors, the EU, the United States and others, meet regularly, twice a year. It's also where we lay the groundwork for solving challenges between the parties. This does not mean that Norway is holding back legitimate criticism. In our contacts with Israeli and Palestinian authorities, we are clear that international law must be respected. Israel is well aware of Norwegian views on the settlements. A possible consequence of the draft representative could be that our balanced approach is being called into question. Restrictive measures aimed at one party can be interpreted as choosing a side of the conflict. If that happens, it could reduce our ability to influence the parties and make the negotiating trail even more demanding. The content of the opinions delivered by the European Court of Justice in November has been well explained, both by the rapporteur and others. I will not repeat too much of what is stated in the reply letter I sent to Stortinget, but, as I wrote in the reply, the various aspects of this matter are for consideration by the government. That assessment process also includes deciding whether there are any particular considerations that suggest that Norwegian legislation should derogate from the corresponding EU legislation in this particular case. In the meeting with the changing context on the ground, we still have to make difficult adjustments. In situations of conflict such as this, there is often a demanding balance where different measures and instruments to put pressure on the parties need to be assessed against each other. The government is working to get the parties back to the negotiating table. That's the overall objective of our work, especially in AHLC. It was said by the representative Petter Eide that little attention was paid to the political situation. I mean, on the contrary, that's what we're doing. We place great emphasis on the political situation and the very special and unique role Norway has, which can contribute positively to the situation.

ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e1589	The proposal we are discussing today is for the labelling of goods produced in Israeli-controlled areas on the West Bank and the Golan Heights. The intention is clear, a desire to limit or prohibit economic relations with Israeli-controlled areas because of, among other things, the fact that there is a need to limit or prohibit economic relations with Israeli-controlled areas. claims that trade in Israeli settlements could be a violation of international law. Professor Kantorovitz of the Northwestern University School of Law has carefully assessed these questions against the perspective of international law. He points out that it has been common practice for countries to practice full opening for economic contact with areas under prolonged occupation or illegal recruitment. No country is obliged to block that type of activity, to require special labelling of goods or to prevent foreign assistance to such areas under international law. Freedom to introduce a boycott of goods produced in any territory is granted if it does not violate free trade agreements or other trade rules. But then it's for diplomatic or political reasons, not for legal reasons. We can also note a 2014 Supreme Court ruling in England that stated that Israeli economic activity on the West Bank does not violate international humanitarian law, and if it should do so, nevertheless, exports of relevant products will be lawful under international law under the Supreme Court in England. Groups in France claim that SodaStream branded its products falsely with the label ‘Made in Israel’ when parts of the shower machine were produced in an Israeli food park on the West Bank. This was rejected by a French court. It is therefore important to note that when a propose boycott of Israel, this is not done for legal reasons, but for political or other reasons. Then there is no need to conceal that Israel has clear improvement points in the enforcement of human rights. Israel is far from some perfect nation, but the country has mechanisms to strengthen human rights, functions that do not exist in other nations in the Middle East. In addition, the labelling of goods which in the next round could lead to the boycott of goods from the West Bank and the Golan Heights will primarily affect Palestinians. Many Palestinians depend entirely on being able to work in companies owned by Israeli companies to serve for their lives. That is actually the reality today. The Christian People's Party is working for a two-state solution that can create a new future for the Palestinians and secure borders for Israel. In our view, it is not the way to go about implementing measures as proposed today, which will quickly strengthen hatred and intransigence to the nation of the Palestinians as their closest neighbour.

ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e1895	The National Audit Board has carried out an investigation into infrastructure and support functions for the operational capability of combat aircraft, which concludes with serious criticism. This purchase is the largest individual public procurement ever in Norway, and it has major economic consequences, not least for the Defence. The estimated lifetime cost of the fighter aircraft is 276 billion. kr, but with the errors revealed in the US National Audit Report, plus the dollar rate, the figure could be significantly larger than this. That is why Red has proposed that the matter should be discussed and dealt with openly at Stortinget as far as is sound for safety reasons. The U.S. National Audit Report, published in several rounds, reveals serious deficiencies in the fighter aircraft and its support functions. There was an open congress hearing in the United States about these deficiencies. Among the deficiencies, there are parts that break faster than promised, a maintenance system that works so poorly that the planes cannot take off, and the cannons cannot manage to shoot properly. So the question is, why are Norwegians going to get less information from our authorities than the Americans get from their own? It's the inhabitants who pay for these planes, not us at Stortinget. That is also why Red believes that we should get a report with up-to-date information – which is apparently available, according to the Council of State – and that it should be published. It will also be in line with the rules deriving from the National Safety Authority’s guidance that the safety rating should not be made more extensive than is necessary and that it is fully feasible, as set out. the same supervisor, to show which parts of a report have which grading or no grading. It is appropriate to ensure that as much information as possible becomes available. It is also the precedent for this, cf. what happened in the 2018 Stortinget control case. Trust is something politicians can't demand, but what we deserve, and keeping secret reports is something that probably undermines trust, not least in a matter that has so much to do with costs and consequences for our entire defence. Red has changed the wording of the two loose proposals at the request and at the suggestion of SV, so that we now ask the presidency to change the mandate of the so-called Harberg Committee, and we hope that we can achieve unanimity in the Stortinge around the two proposals at least.

ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e2047	Since the Committee does not agree on either the description of reality or the solutions, the comments on behalf of the Committee will be brief. The suggestions in this setting have been addressed up to several times by Stortinget in the past. As the heading of the proposers suggests, there are proposals for the reversal of changes in welfare schemes, some way back to 2011, when one of the proposers himself was in government. That is why I do not comment on all the issues, but first I want to correct an incurrency. Reference is made to the 2018 budget agreement on raising the roof of child supplements from 95 pst. until 99 p.m., it does not mean that it has been done for the current financial year. The Norwegian welfare society is at all times more than the sum of public benefits. It is the opportunity to learn through play in kindergarten, provide opportunities for everyone through a good public school, have a decent and inclusive working life, a good health care and, of course, a welfare policy that takes care of us when for various reasons we cannot do this ourselves. The value of the welfare state is not least now, when we are in the midst of a crisis that affects both life, health, our own and the common economy. But the dilemma of welfare policy is both to provide a social security network that keeps us down, but does not stick to us. When the Right is talking about a sustainable welfare state, it's about the welfare state's ability to carry those who need it in the periods they need it, also in the future. Then it's a little motivating that all debates, all changes, all system criticism are not only stamped as unsocial, but also attempted as if the critics think this is because of the individuals themselves. Of course, the last one is wrong. The first prevents a constructive debate on improving the complex system of well-intentioned benefits and arrangements we have in Norway. When the government of Solberg joined in 2013, the heirs of the Workers' Party, the Central Party and the Socialist Left were longer health cows and 15 000 more poor children than when the three aforementioned parties joined. In 2013, there were also nearly 70000 recipients of redundancy benefits in the process of leaving the scheme because it did not function properly. So some of the first government Solberg had to do was extend the benefits while we tried to find answers to what was wrong, and what had to be done. When the scheme was evaluated, a work life researcher at the Frischsenteret, Simen Markussen, said: ‘AAP is a treadmill out of work, where it is accepted that you cannot work and where people are locked inside the outside closet’. Since then, several changes have been made to the scheme, such as tightening up what is needed to qualify and extending the possibility of trying to work without losing the right to benefit – both to target the scheme and not to lock people into poverty. After the changes, we've seen an increase of some more who are going to social assistance than before, as well as faster clarification of disability for those who are entitled to this, and more back in activity and work, but we haven't been close to sending 70,000 people into uncertainty, as it was in 2013. It is not unsocial to make the necessary changes to schemes that act as a treadmill out of work. On the contrary, it's unsocial to leave out. The same applies to changes in the default reform adopted in 2011 and implemented in 2015. We know that many people are out of work despite both their willingness to work and their ability to work. Therefore, benefits and arrangements must be designed in such a way that there are no economic barriers to participation. The changes have made it easier to combine work and social security, become fairer and have given a net higher social security for the majority. I think it's unsocial to reverse the changes. I understand that it may seem unreasonable when making changes to our many welfare benefits. Some welfare schemes may have been right to introduce when they arrived but no longer work as intended or are out of date. Since women did not have a high level of participation, it may have been right to have long transitional arrangements, so that those who have been cared for by the partner could change after a break. But by 2020, we need to be able to change and phase out such schemes, even if it seems unfair to those who receive them. With the Right in government, kindergartens and the SFO have become cheaper for those who need it the most. Several children can take part in leisure activities and child care is increasing. Until now, there has been a decline in health cows. The queues for help for people in rushes have been reduced, and perhaps the biggest social reform in recent times has been adopted, namely the drug reform. When society changes, it also needs to change the device of public welfare. And again, the sum of the Norwegian welfare community is greater than the sum of the social benefits, fortunately.

ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e2343	I would like to begin by thanking the proposers for the matter and the Committee for good cooperation. From the outset, there has been a broad majority in favour of a system of a Nav Ombudsman, but slightly different opinions about what an ombudsman implies, and what kind of device it should have. It is not so strange, as there is no clear definition of what an agent is, and the hearings confirmed the case in this context. I will therefore try to explain what the majority, consisting of the government parties and the Progress Party, has stressed in this matter. It is important for trust in welfare systems to ensure legal certainty for users and the ability to complain about decisions and services. There are already statutory legal security arrangements in the management of Nav, and for us it has been important to ensure that a system of a Nav Ombudsman becomes a real strengthening of the legal security of the user and not just another body to be dealt with, which further complicates the situation of the user and is perceived as a new bottleneck. It is also important to point out that the establishment of a Nav ombudsman cannot and should not be the solution to all Nav challenges. For example, several Hearing Officers have found that Nav’s guidance obligation is not being complied with and that there is a significant need to improve it. It is important that these signals are taken seriously in Nav and that today's legislation, which already provides good guidance from the administration and several complaints, is respected. It has therefore been important for us to point out that it is not intended that a Nav Ombudsman should assume the obligation to provide guidance or other obligations under the Administration Act that is the responsibility of Nav and Social Security. It could have acted quickly against its purpose and undermined existing rights and seemed more bureaucratic. It is important that the Ombudsman becomes an unbureaucratic and user-friendly system and that it acts as a legal security guarantee that checks that users receive the right service at the right time. We have therefore concluded that an ombudsman can best function as a system under the Ombudsman of the Administration of Storting, the Ombudsman of Civil Liberties. The Ombudsman already deals with a number of issues from the labour and welfare management and has good systems of contact with Nav users and people who need guidance in complaint. The civil ombudsman's assessments are also of high professional quality and are considered to be objective and impartial. In addition, the Ombudsman enjoys authority in administration and high respect for the population. By bringing together the Ombudsman functions of Nav with the Civil Ombudsman, responsibility for monitoring and follow-up is also avoided between different representatives, oversight and arrangements. It is also important to point out that a prerequisite for the proper functioning of the Ombudsman with this device is the provision of sufficient competence and capacity to carry out a real check. With that, I address our proposals in the matter, but I would like to clarify that in proposal No. 1 the reference is replaced by the following: ‘The size calls on the Presidency to change the mandate of the sample investigating the control functions of the Grand Duchy so that the sample assesses and makes proposals for the establishment of a Nav Ombudsman in line with the comments of the Grand majority of the Innst. [[Harberg Committee]] 254 S for 2019-2020.’

ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e2368	Thank you, Mr President-in-Office, and thank you to the rapporteurs. The question of a Nav ombudsman is an important matter, especially in the light of the Nav scandal, which the government itself has chosen to call it. On 5 March of this year, the Stortinget examined the matter from the Control and Constitutional Committee on the presentation by the Minister for Employment and Social Affairs of 5 November 2019 in the Stortinget on the mispractice of the EU Social Security Regulation. The Council of State itself said in that debate that several questions should have been raised earlier. The Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs was not satisfied. The error had been going on for many years. The council further said that Nav should have caught up with the error earlier, but no one thus picked up the error before the Social Security Court started looking at this in 2017. And yet the wrong practice continued for years. People got an unfair refund decision against themselves. People were reported and convicted for something they should not have been reported and convicted for. As others discussed on March 5, an important feature of Norwegian society is a high level of trust between population and government. This trust has not come about by itself, but has been built on stone by political decisions in the development of the rule of law and the Norwegian welfare community for more than 100 years. That trust is our responsibility as elected representatives to take care of. It's just that rug that makes the Nav scandal so serious. It is now absolutely necessary that we manage to re-enter the trust of Nav. The Labour Party therefore supports the creation of a Nav Ombudsman, but we do not think it alone is enough. It must be stronger than that, but it can be one of several contributions in the right direction. It is not the first time the Stortinget is dealing with the matter of establishing a Nav Ombudsman. There have been several suggestions from several batches on both sides of the distinction between today's position and opposition. But what has been a constant move to now has been that the proposals have been promoted from the opposition and voted down by the position. This was also the case when Stortinget dealt with the matter in connection with the SV’s proposal for a representative in December 2014. Then there was also a proposal from the Workers' Party to ask the government to assess the establishment of a Nav ombudsman as soon as possible, voted down, among others. by the Progress Party, which is behind the proposal we are debating today. All the parties to the Committee will now have an agent. That's good. But at the same time, we have a slightly different view of how an agent should be organised and operated. The Government Parties and the Advance Party have decided. They want the order added to the Civil Ombudsman. The Workers' Party is sceptical because, as the Council also points out in its letter to the Committee, a large proportion of the Civil Ombudsman's portfolio is concerned with matters relating to precisely labour and welfare management. So we think it's easy to get a mix of functions. We also believe that there are good arguments to claim that one central body can easily become less accessible to people who have issues going on in Nav and who already experience that it is difficult to reach. So we want a thorough assessment of what's the best way to do this. We're rather in the direction of state-owned agents, maybe even more in some of the largest states, both in the population and in the area. For some of the points of an agent, it must be that it is available to those who need assistance from there. SV wants to organise according to the model of the Patient and User Ombudsman, and the Workers' Party sees that there may be good reasons for this. The center part keeps this a little bit more open, just like we do. Finally, it must be allowed to note that the matter appears a bit strange. The majority – I then assume that the Left in this case is in agreement with the Progress Party and the other government parties – despite the fact that the Council of State says he will investigate the matter, as stated in the Granavolden Declaration, has actually taken the matter out of the government's hands and added it to the Stortinget. What also seems a little strange, is that the Progress Party did not take the opportunity to get a case on the Nav Ombudsman while they were still in government, but then promote proposals in the Stortinget only two weeks after they chose to leave the government. It seems that there has been some kind of towing either internally in the government or between the government and the government parties in the Storting in this matter. Now we no longer have a majority government, and that's maybe what saves the case and makes us get a majority of representatives this time? ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e2392	First of all, I would like to thank both the rapporteur and the other parties in the Committee on the Verification of Credentials and the Committee on Legal Affairs and Citizens' Rights. It is eleven years since the Progress Party first proposed the creation of a Nav ombudsman. Many other proposals by the Progress Party have also been criticised before they were adopted, but then it ends up that most are finally in favour of the proposals anyway. The creation of a Nav ombudsman is a matter that everyone has become after the Progress Party has proposed it. The existence of control mechanisms in society and the ability of some to speak on behalf of users are an important principle. Just as we have an senior agent or a patient and user agent, it is important that we have an agent for those who meet faceless machine bureaucracy. The Nav scandal last year showed that the system is not perfect. Although for all those who were unjustly convicted, there is a lean comfort in having an agent now to secure the rights of users, we will be better equipped to ensure that such things do not happen in the future. I believe that a Nav agent, who is going to go after the bureaucracy in the seas, who speaks the case of the users and guarantees them the rights they have, is over-mature in order to be adopted. As has been pointed out earlier, the case has been at the hearing, and so to speak, all the hearing bodies gave a positive opinion on the setting up of the Ombudsman – there were different opinions on how it should be established, correct. It was also found that there is a great need and a great desire in the population for an agent who can speak their case, and relevant stakeholder organisations actively go out and support the creation of the Ombudsman. It's positive, and it strengthens my belief that it's right to set up a Nav agent. The Ombudsman is intended to be an unbureaucratic and user-friendly representative. A very large organisation shall not be set up and shall not take over Nav's proceedings. Nor shall it take over cases on behalf of the Social Security Court. I believe it is important for the Ombudsman to be responsible for both the municipal and state parts of Nav. That's because it's not going to be broken up and divided. It is important that a Nav ombudsman is an agent for everyone on Nav, and that there is no distinction between who pays for the welfare goods. For the users of Nav, it is also irrelevant; they only want the service they require. The Ombudsman would obviously be independent and impartial. That means that it is not the organisation you complain about, which is also the one you complain to. Users can therefore be sure that they are met by someone who does not have a pre-intentional and possibly a negative approach, and that their case is handled correctly. On that basis and as described in the comments in the case, we, the Progress Party, in cooperation with the Government Parties, believe that a Nav Ombudsman is best placed to establish himself as a system under the Ombudsman of the Administration of the Storting, the Civil Ombudsman. I am glad that on Stortinget there is a willingness to set up a Nav Ombudsman, although I see that several parties will investigate and in that way also postpone the matter. My ambition is that the Ombudsman be created next year, and that the government will check out any legal issues as soon as possible. I assume that the government will work quickly on this matter and set up the Ombudsman as soon as possible. Finally, I would like to thank both the government parties and the other parties in the Chamber for their attendance and cooperation in this matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e2882	As others have pointed out, this is a discussion that has been going on for several years. In particular, the discussion continues, for everyone seems to agree that it may be a good idea to have a Nav agent and to investigate a Nav agent, it also says in the government platform. But then there's been a discussion about how, and in what way. Let me just say first of all that the fact that we have legal certainty and the possibility to complain about both decisions and services is very important for the trust in our welfare systems. The background to the proposal for a Nav ombudsman is the desire to strengthen the legal certainty of users and, at the same time, contribute to more user-friendly management and provide an enhanced opportunity to point out failures or deficiencies in the system. In the EEA case, the issue has naturally been up-to-date, as there have been timely questions about how users' interests and legal certainty are safeguarded within current systems and mechanisms. As an overall committee points out, there is a clear majority of those heard in this case who support the proposal for a separate Nav Ombudsman. However, as I said, it is worth noting that there are a lot of different opinions about what a Nav ombudsman should be like, how it should be organised, and what kind of mandate it should have. What is important to say is that there is a broad consensus that a Nav ombudsman should not be a substitute for the statutory legal security systems of the labour and welfare management. Users shall continue to use the formal complaints in the first place. With regard to State benefits and services, there is a right of appeal before the Nav Board of Appeal, with appeal before the Social Security Court. Complaints about social services decisions shall continue to be treated independently by the State. In addition, it is possible to bring the case before the Court after the said appeals and appeals have been exhausted. It should also be possible for users to use the service complaint mechanism established in Nav, where users can make complaints about the service they have received, and where the administration deals with and responds to complaints about the service received. The legal certainty of users is primarily ensured by effective and well-functioning management. Then we also have, as an independent supervisory body outside the administration, the Civil Ombudsman. They receive complaints from those who believe they are subject to injustice or maladministration, and they deal with complaints concerning both state, county and municipal administration. Of the total number of issues, issues related to labour and welfare management are a significant proportion. Then I have to hurry to add that it does not automatically mean that the Civil Ombudsman has a high proportion of complaints that they deal with and agree with, but that means that the Civil Ombudsman has an important role as a welfare representative already. The majority in the UK has then decided to investigate how the Ombudsman can strengthen his/her role, competence and capacity as representative of the users of the labour and welfare management. It might be wise to do, but historically it's a little bit special as a decision, because normally it's the government that's taking the investigation. But precisely because the Civil Ombudsman is another type of institution outside the administration, Stortinget has chosen a different solution, as the representatives of the majority parties have explained. The Ombudsman is, of course, the Ombudsman for the administration of the Storting, and therefore the majority of the parties have chosen to ask the Presidency to give the mandate to the committee which is now going through the control functions of the Storting. It also means that with such a decision, the government will not come back with an investigation, because if the presidency wants it and it is adopted, it is the Harberg Committee that the Storting itself has set up, that will investigate it. Otherwise, of course, we will follow what the majority decides.

ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e3031	This case concerns proposals from the government on changes to the Patient Injury Act, which provides the Ministry of Health and Welfare with legal support for the provision of a separate regulation on the measurement of men’s compensation in the event of patient injury. ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e3042	Let me first thank the case rapporteur for a good job in the matter. If you get damage to the digestive system, if you get damage to your genitals, or if you get some sort of pain condition, you are at risk today that the amount of men’s compensation you get depends on who is handling the application. This is because the legislation we have today is designed in the light of typical occupational injuries. In the disability table for occupational injuries, not all of these disorders are included, and these are just some of the examples. Measurements may also be different despite the fact that the damage results in a similar disability. Right will require equal assessment of their applications for men’s replacement, regardless of where they live and who is dealing with the application. That is why the government has also taken this into account, and in this case we will probably give the government permission to make a separate provision for the payment of men's compensation for patients' injuries. In this way, we can also ensure that people receive equal treatment. For the Right, there is now no time to sit and wait for new investigations and new postponements for those who are now entitled to predictability and non-discrimination in these kinds of cases. I also want to comment briefly on something that comes from the Workers' Party. As indicated in the proposal, the government and Norwegian patient compensation agree that compensation for injury should be measured equally, irrespective of the basis of responsibility, and that a common regulation and revision of the disability table are possible and can be used for the entire patient injury area. There is nothing to prevent this later being grouped together in a common regulation or in a common table. It also writes the Government Parties and the Advance Party may be appropriate to consider in the long term, while an overall review, which is also addressed, will delay the work of non-discrimination for persons who are required to have paid but compensation.
ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e3059	Patients who are significantly and permanently injured as a result of failure to health care are entitled to men’s compensation. The calculation of men’s replacements is far on the road standardised. Any person with a particular disease or injury shall be assigned the same percentage of disability. The sum of the compensation is based on a fixed percentage of the basic amount of the public interest. Determination of the degree of invalidity and the calculation of compensation in the event of injury to patients are based on the occupational injury regulation and the corresponding invalidity table. The legislation is designed primarily in view of conditions arising from occupational injury. The disability table has been slightly updated in the course of medical development and is deficient for patient injuries. I believe it is important that it is easy to get an overview of how men’s compensation should be measured in case of patient injury. However, compensation for personal injury should be measured in the same way as possible, irrespective of the basis of liability. Therefore, the best thing would have been a common regulation and a joint revision of the disability table. I believe that it is not realistic to get to this within a reasonable time. I believe that the need to put in place a transparent regulation on patients' injuries means that there should be a legal framework for a separate regulation on the provision of men's compensation for patients' injuries. This does not prevent the rules from subsequently being grouped into a common regulation or the provision of a common disability table. I do not want the calculation of compensation to be detached from the general right to compensation and the law on compensation for damages. The statutory home should also include rules derogating from the Law on Compensation for Damage. The Regulation shall ensure equal treatment of patients' injuries, predictability of the calculations of men's replacements for the patient and that the replacement is based on up-to-date medical knowledge. Several hearing bodies believe that the new regulation will restrict the rights of men’s compensation in the event of patient injury. I do not agree with that presentation. The draft regulation essentially implies a continuation and clarification of the law in force. In addition, I have proposed that there should be some way to derogate from the general rules where this would lead to an unreasonable outcome for the patient. Having said that, I will of course carefully consider the objections of the hearing bodies in the further work on the regulation. I am pleased that the majority of the committee supports the government's proposal for a legal basis for a separate regulation on the calculation of the compensation of men and women in the event of injury to patients, which can also complement and derogate from the rules of the Claims Act.

This is the back carpet. It's these challenges we have to solve in the future, and it requires us to think differently about a lot. We don't have enough people in this country to do that. So we have to think differently. And then we have to be better at using each other's competences more effectively. In several hospitals, they are already working in teams. That's a good way to make sure that everyone gets the best use of their skills, so we have to do more of that. They are close to the patient, provide good care and have a lot of whole work around the patient. They can help relieve the caregivers so that they have fewer tasks.
