We cannot solve all the challenges we face in the health sector without using new technologies. At Kalne's hospital in Eastfold, new technology has been overcome with the problem of mismedication of patients. By scanning patients and medical containers, it's almost impossible to give the patient the wrong medicine. There are many ways to use new technologies to provide better healthcare for the patient. The hospital treatment of the future does not have to be done inside the hospital itself. For some patients it may be a burden to go to the hospital and get treatment there. It can be small children who have the best at home. There may be people with a long route, or people who are vulnerable to infections. We have to look at the hospitals as more than just building. By moving treatment home to patients, we give patients freedom, and we give them more and better services. An example of this is the Nordlands Hospital, which is now following up dialysis patients at home. Patients can receive treatment at home instead of going to the hospital several times a week. The patient records measurements of an app on the mobile while automatically obtaining data from weight and blood pressure measurements. This helps the kidney nurse in the hospital to better understand the condition of the patient than when it is collected manually. We're going to talk more about personalised medicine in the future. This plan is about the content of healthcare, how to deliver even better services through better use of our expertise, better cooperation and better use of new technologies. ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-12.ana.ud252e3397	National health and hospital plan is the government’s strategy to provide the patient’s healthcare in a sustainable way. The first national health and hospital plan clarified the hospital structure in Norway. We are now setting out the direction and framework for the development of specialist healthcare and cooperation with municipal health and care services. When I presented the message last November, covid-19 was not yet discovered. The management of the virus has naturally taken most of the attention both in healthcare and in society in recent months, and it is likely to affect us in the next few years as well. It can then be easy to imagine that the National Health and Hospital Plan, which the Stortinge treats today, is out of date. That's not true. I'd rather say the opposite. The challenges that we addressed in the National Health and Hospital Plan are still there. We're getting more, we're getting older, and we expect more of the health care. At the same time, there is a limit to how much more health professionals we can realistically access. This makes it difficult to reconcile wishes and opportunities within the resources available to us. The pandemic we're now in doesn't change this picture. It enhances the image. There are many challenges, but there are also a lot of opportunities. In order to achieve the patient’s health care in a sustainable way, we need to exploit the opportunities offered by technology, make best use of the skills of employees and solve the problems in new ways. With one of the best health services in the world, we have a good starting point. In the patient’s healthcare system, the patient should be able to be an active participant in his or her own treatment, but also in the development of healthcare services. Patient and dependants shall be seen and heard. Communication between patients and healthcare should be understandable. Competence shall be a matter of course and citizens shall be able to communicate with the services digitally. Patients shall experience a coherent healthcare service across hospitals and municipalities. Vulnerable patients with complex needs shall experience a service that occurs in teams around them. The establishment of the 19 health communities shall contribute to a more coherent health and care service and a more equivalent partnership between municipalities and hospitals. In the health community, representatives from municipalities and hospitals, users and permanent residents will jointly develop and plan services for vulnerable patient groups and find solutions tailored to local needs. The number of hospitals and municipalities in the various health communities will vary. The Committee is concerned that non-profit hospitals should be included in the health community, and I fully agree with that. We've always taken this into account. The exact identity of the sample should be agreed locally. The Government and the CS agree that health communities should give priority to four groups in their joint planning: vulnerable elderly people, children and young people, people with severe mental and drug problems and persons with multiple chronic disorders. Crisis management has made it difficult to start formal work on the establishment of the health communities and the concrete planning of services for the four patient groups, but my impression is very clear: much of the cooperation that has emerged during these months around the management of the covid-19 pandemic, both from the municipalities, from the hospitals and with the counties involved, has just been based on the principles of the health communities, and therefore this whole idea has now been put into practice in many places. I am absolutely sure that the experience gained now will be valuable in the further work on establishing a health community and helping bring hospitals and municipalities closer together, and there will also be a strength in the emergency work. In the patient’s healthcare system, it is a goal that specialist health care should get closer to the patient. In the future, hospitals will be less a house the patient comes to, and to a greater extent a service that comes to the patient. We want to create the extroverted hospital where specialists in large hospitals also assist colleagues in smaller hospitals, specialists from hospitals cooperate with local health professionals in the municipal arena, and hospitals help the patient at home or through digital solutions. This is important for the way hospitals act in the health community. Psychiatric health protection is a subject addressed in the notification. We're conducting three main operations. We need to better estimate what the population needs and how services should be organised to meet this need. We will give priority to quality and content. These include: to ask the patient what works, and to introduce what works, faster. We will further develop services by implementing new forms of treatment, digital solutions and more team-based work. In the National Health and Hospital Plan, we set a clear direction for the use of technology in healthcare. We need to exploit the potential of technology to move services home to the patient, use our common health data and artificial intelligence to develop better health care and facilitate more personalised medicine. Moving more services home can contribute to increasing patient mastering, a more customised offer, the early capture of signs of deterioration, and better protection against infection. Less time spent on travel and waiting means more freedom for patients and better resource use in the service. Here again, we see that the use of video and other digital solutions in the service has increased dramatically over the past few weeks as a result of the covid-19 pandemic. In April, I asked health regions to use digital home follow-up and online treatment where possible. We need to ensure that the positive changes that are taking place now become permanent and an integral part of the service even when the crisis is over. The Government will ensure clear governance and coordination of digitisation actions. The patient treatment objectives shall lead to the implementation of digitisation measures. Once again, the emphasis of the message on digitisation has been an important part of the preparedness of this situation. The Covid-19 situation has also underlined the importance of having sufficient health professionals with the right skills to deal with these kinds of crises. In the plan we describe that if we continue as today, according to the SSB, the number of annual works has to increase by 35 pst. versus 2035. In this case, almost half of the growth in the labour force in Norway has to go to health and care. It goes without saying that that's not possible. Therefore, we need to shift resource growth away from manning, which is a scarce resource, to investing in skills and technology, so that we can reduce future needs for increased labour. Hospitals need both personnel and competence planning in order to meet the needs of the services. That means they need to know how many heads they're going to need, and what skills the employees need. There are especially nurses and health professionals we want to lack. These are the groups that receive the most attention in this plan. We will increase the educational capacity of nurses and increase the quality of continuing training. It's a good thing to say today, on the world's day for nurses – and that also gives me a good opportunity to congratulate the President on the day. In the revised national budget, the government proposes 260 million. kr, corresponding to one annual admission of about 4000 more students, of which about 1500 are in health and social subjects. Plasters are distributed among others. medical, nursing and further training in nursing. In the national health and hospital plan, we state that health professionals should have a place in hospitals. Hospitals also have an important responsibility to ensure that enough health professionals are trained for community health and care services. That is why we demand more health professionals and the establishment of training offices in all health businesses. Some may claim that the national health and hospital plan is out of date because it does not cover the emergency area. Here, too, I disagree. The message provides more answers on how to prepare ourselves for future crises. To respond to future challenges, we need enough people with the right skills, close cooperation between municipalities and hospitals, and we need to further develop the emergency medical chain. The plan also mentions national health and preparedness plans as the overall framework for the prevention and management of all types of crises and disasters in the healthcare sector. Any crisis and pandemic will be different, and the need for action will vary, but there will undoubtedly be learning points from the covid-19 pandemic, and of course we have to come back to that. Both municipalities and hospitals are making an impressive effort to deal with the pandemic. The crisis has seized a great deal of capacity both in services and in agencies, which has delayed the implementation of national health and hospital plans. At the same time, this reinforces the need to implement the measures, and it will be an important priority for the government also on the way out of the crisis.

Five days ago, on May 7th, there was, as very often, the crossing of Sennaland, the crossing between Alta and Hammerfest, a mountain crossing that has had 48 closures and 86 column runs from 1 October last year until today. In addition, the distance is rv. 94 from Kvalsund to Hammerfest had 30 closures and 16 column runs during the same period. Their granddaughter came to Alta near-sickness as a first-born and already had a 2 cm opening. The midwife tried to get hold of the ambulance, but they were already on their way to Hammerfest with other patients, and no one was available. The midwife then asks them to drive by private car to Hammerfest, a distance of 140 km, in storms of collapsing, without a midwife or any other health care professional available, only a anxious young father who has enough to follow the rear lights of the bridge car. The young grandfather had never crossed Sennaland before. They wait for the next column and don't know when it's going, while the young growing mother has stronger and stronger riding. They were alive at home, too. Old mother felt totally powerless and went over 4,000 steps in the living room before the young couple reached Hammerfest. You never know what can happen to a firstborn. When the young couple came to Akkarfjord, the water went, and understandably they became even saviors. Luckily, they got there. Then the birth was well-timed, and they had to put the epidural immediately. The situation was very critical. Prime Minister Solberg, in his New Year's speech in 2019, called on the Nordics to make more children, and the Prime Minister felt there was reason to ask whether it was too difficult to have children in the past. Now I've described the reality of Finnmark in 2020, a reality that makes many women hesitate to give birth to more children – a situation that is all too often the rule and not the exception. It's a lot of luck that made this go well. The only prayer the newly-baked grandmother would I have to convey is, please, let's get the maternity ward in Alta. This is madness.
